146. Natives Adr itted to Certain Government
Positions.
Hanoi, July 20.- The Government Gonoral of
French Indochina on July 1l promulgated a degree
granting native inhabitants the privilege to occupy
certain government positions in special administrative
districts in Saigon and Cholon as well as in other
cities, towns and villages which heretofore had been
held by the French alone. The positions made available
to the natives by virtue of the decree are chief and
assistant secretaries, engineers, financial inspectors,
and health inspectors in case of srecial administrative
districts in Saigon and Cholon. With regard to directive
organs in cities, towns and villages, administrative,
technical, financial and health positions have been
opened. The present action is a sequel to the recently
adopted policy of opening government positions to native
inhabitants, increasing their salaries and improving the
treatment of recruited native troops. (Domei in English).
147. Collection of Toll Tax from Thai Residents
Abolished.
Hanoi, July 2l.- A regulation stipulating
collection of poll taxes from Thai residents in Cambodia
and Laos as well as in other districts in French Indochina
was abolished under a deoree issued by Governor General
Jean Decoux under the date of July 7. It was understood
that hitherto Thai residents have been taxed on the same
basis as native inhabitants. (Domei in English).
148. Report by Domei in Chinese of Indochina
Developments.
Saigon, July 22.- Since the conclusion of the
armistice between Germany and France in the current war,
Japan has been the only country through which French
Indochina could trade. And since the conclusion of the
Franco-Jananese pact of May 6, 1041, she has been the
foundation stone upon which the economic relationship
of the two countries was based. From Indochina's point
of view, the direct advantage of the pact was that no
restrictive measures are necessary to limit production.
with respect to imports, Indochina's smooth and stable
economic life used to depend on a large amount of imports
from different places in East Asia. However, due to the
sudden change brought about by the outbreak of the
Greater East Asia War on December 8, 194l, a new problem
arose--communications with France were cut and trade with
couhtries on the Pacific coast came to a standstill.
Hence, Japan became the only country with which Indochina
could trade.
The Indochinese authorities proclaimed different
regulations with regard to the progress of control measures
in an attempt to readjust the economic system. On August 13,
1941, the Indochinese authorities proolaimed that the
import and export of all commodities were permissible and
that the import corporations should organise a guild to
aot for the authorities in matters relating to the
proeedure of imports. The same guild system was applied
to

=== Page 96 === (confidence 0.99)
-62-
to exports. Owing to the importance of rubber and
grains, their export control was very strict. A
special organization dealing exclusively with the
purchase and exnort of rubber was therefore established
in February, 194l, in Saigon under the direct super-
vision of the authorities and the advice of experts.
Rice and corn are under the control and management of
a speoial organization. On December 1, 1942, this
organ was reorganized into the grain committee, directly
attached to the Ministry of Economics. Besides holding
the responsibility of making contr cts for the delivery
of nolished and non-polished rice and corn, that com-
mittee is authorized to order all guilds in the country
to conduct the business of transportatior, purchese,
sale and delivery of grain.
Referring to actual conditions under which distri-
butions are made in southern Indochina, measures intro-
duced in December, 1941, emphasized...consumption of
important products. Most of the imrorted goods were
under direct governmentel control and special distribut-
ing committees were established. As cloth weaving is
the most imnortent industry in all the homes in Indochina,
a ration system for cotton thread, rayon and artificial
cotton was immediately installed after the establishment
of the weaving association. Paper is also controlled
in accordance with the production situation. A marked
improvement has been mede in the control of matches,
sugar, soap, timber, fats and alcohol. Control of
production is observed at the same time with control
of foreign trade and general consumption as its purpose
is to check over-production at home.
In the case of minerals, it is especially necessary
to control production in view of reduced exportation and
small demand in the country. Production plans for neces-
sary industries have changed so much that develonment
projects must always be kept up.
Indochinese authorities have done their utmost to
develop agriculture and are particularly keen in the
production of textiles and oils. Before the war, Indo-
china imported 15,000 tons each of cotton and cotton
thread and 20,000 tons of yellow flax. At that time,
the annual production of cotton in Indochina was about
1,000 tons and of yellow flax only a few hundred tons.
Consequently it was decided to increase the production
of cotton and oil. A plan for increasing the production
of sesame oil is presently underway. It is expected that
oil-producing plants will be planted everywhere in the
country.
In the fiold of industry, in order to make Indochina
self-sufficient, light industries have been encouraged
and considerably expanded. Due to the development of
manufacture of alcohol, charcoal gas and generators,
four (?) percent of last year's motor cars are kept
runningl
Speaking of priee sontrol, Indochina used to be a
relatively inexpensive country, but at sueh a time when
prioee

=== Page 97 === (confidence 0.99)
-63-
.j country are rising and imnorted goods
ipensive and the demand exceeds supply, it
matural that prices in Indochina also went up.
ore it was decided to control all prices in the
ry. Prices for all commodities are required to be
a and conomic police" have been formed to prevent
Ppossible violation. The Central Price Control Com-
Jee is responsible for carrying out the rules and
Glations. Its chairman will be either the head of the
Aistry of Economics or the person acting in his capacity
d the vice chairman will be chosen from among the offi-
ials directly concerned with the different guilds. At
resent all guilds are in agreement with the policy of
the authorities. Following are the six guilds organized
since the control system came into effect: minerals guild;
industrial guild; transportation guild; agricultural guild;
commeroial guild and finance guild. Although they may be
of different sizes, they are all under the control of the
governor. Connection between all these guilds are very
close and, with a view to facilitating their activities,
an Indochina economic association was formed with the
head of the bureau of economics as its chairman and repre-
sentatives of the guilds and the governor as its members
so that connection may be meintained through the joint
association of the guilds. (Domei in Chinese).
149. Construction of Large Fishing Port near Saigon.
Saigon, July 25.- Construction of a large fishing
port at Binh Dinh in the neighborhood of Cap St. Jacques,
outer harbor of Saigon, has now entered the second stage fol-
lowing the completion of the foundation work. The port, which
is expected to be completed by the beginning of next year,
will become the center for surnlying fresh fish as well as
fish processing and oil manufacturing. The construction
will be started shortly of another fishing port at Vinh, nor-
thern Annam. (Domei in Enelish).

=== Page 374 === (confidence 0.98)
EMORANDULi:
The Japanese local broadcasts for the Netherlands
Indies from Bandoeng, Lacassar and Batavia are now being
regularly received in Australia and provide important
material for military purposes because the Japanese are
not aware that these broadcasts are being received.
information obtained fron these broadcasts, therefore, is
distributed in a strictly confidential mnner with the
request that it not be used in such a way that the fact
of its recertion be betrayed.
Tublicatior of inforuntion fron the Japanese
hone broadcasting service in the United Stotes and also
in the shortwave broadcasts has alroady caused complete
disaprearance of all military, economical, and psychological
indiscretions in these broadcasts, which previously occurred
frequently. The Japanese can also prevent the reception
of these broadcasts in the nited States and in Australia
by weakering their volume.
The Halay broadcast of Radio San Francisco of
June 26th at 23.10 F.X. mentioned the fact that the Tojo
Government also has a brordcasting staticn at Yacassar
which continuelly nakes exasgerated stateuents concerning
Japanese victories and completely false news. Furthermore
Radio Macessar local service has been cuoted about anti-
aircraft exercises. T
These fects are of serious consequence
because the Sar Francisco shortwave broadcasts very probably
cannot be reccived by the ropulation of the East Indies
but are most certainly listened in on by the Japanese.

=== Page 375 === (confidence 0.99)
randum Continued--2
Therefore it is very urgent that no reference be made in
American broadcasts or in publications to local Japanese
broadcasts in the Hetherlands Indies, even if they are
directly received in the United States.
This question also has the attention of the
military authorities in Australia.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADVISER ON POLITICAL RELATIONS
July 23, 1943.
CI/L - Mr. Myers:
Reference, Mr. George Taylor's memorandum
to you of July 2l in regard to the subject of
broadcasts to which the Netherlands Ambassador
had called attention in the Ambassador's letter
to me of July 14:
In Mr. Taylor's memorandum there 1s given
what appears to me an adequate explanation of
the broadcast of June 6 under reference and an
ample rejoinder to the 1mplied complaint trans-
mitted by the Ambassador. However, exnlanation
and justification, while serving to throw
complsint out of court, do not, in my opinion,
go to the heart of the matter. The important
question, it seeme to me, is: Is it or is it
not advisable for us (or the Dutch) in any way
to call attention to the fact that we are
aware of the exietence of the Jananese broad-
casting station at Macassar and that broadcasts
from that station are being monitored in
Australia by the Dutch? In raising that ques-
tion, I am hot attempting to give the answer
to it. But, to me, it seems to be the question
which neede moet to be given consideration.
S
(is. 9 ane attutin)

My dear Mr. Mly:

informs me that it is anxious to obtain inoreased coverage
of the Japanese domestic and empire broadoasts. It be-
lieves that the material to be obtained from these twe
sets of broadeasts would be of great value in assessing

various aspeots of the war. The domestic broadoasts

fore, be extremely interested if you could arrange to ex-

tions.

Replying to your letter of arch 3rd, urging our
Foreign Broadcast intelligence Service to obtain increased
coverage of the Japanese domestic and ompire broadcasts,
we are moving to obtain this increased coverage as rapidly
as the dirfioult translator situation and financial con-

the nawaiian Islands to intercept tre medium wave broad-
